response","url":"https://github.com/quarkiverse/quarkus-cxf/issues/2240","totalCommentsCount":17,"viewerCanUpdateNext":false,"viewerCanSetStatus":false,"subIssuesSummary":{"total":0,"completed":0},"state":"CLOSED","stateReason":"COMPLETED","status":null,"duplicateOf":null,"locked":false,"viewerCanComment":false,"databaseId":5017734428,"createdAt":"2026-07-30T08:19:59Z","viewerDidAuthor":false,"author":{"avatarUrl":"https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/19404874?u=c3e03669fc8feb128f76742423702c0355893655\u0026v=4","login":"cj19","id":"MDQ6VXNlcjE5NDA0ODc0","profileUrl":"https://github.com/cj19","name":"Roland Darvas","__typename":"User"},"body":"## Description\n\nWhen a CXF client using the default `VertxHttpClientHTTPConduitFactory` experiences\na `receive-timeout` (`quarkus.cxf.client.\"client-name\".receive-timeout`) while under\nconcurrent load, some connections are not returned to the Vert.x HTTP connection\npool. Once enough of these accumulate, the pool becomes permanently stuck at its\nconfigured maximum (`quarkus.cxf.client.\"client-name\".vertx.connection-pool.http1-max-size`)\nand never recovers, even after all client traffic stops. New requests to the same\ndownstream client then queue indefinitely / fail, effectively blocking that CXF\nclient for the lifetime of the JVM process (only a restart recovers it).\n\nThis was investigated as a suspected root cause of a production incident where an\napplication's outbound SOAP client stopped being able to reach a downstream service\nafter a period of that downstream service being slow/unresponsive, even once the\ndownstream service recovered.\n\n## Actual behavior\n\nAcross repeated runs, on both Quarkus 3.26.2 and 3.37.3, `http_client_active_connections`\nclimbs to the configured pool max (20 in our tests) under load, as expected. In\nmost runs it recovers to 0 within roughly 55-90 seconds after load stops — consistent\nwith the default `quarkus.cxf.client.\"client-name\".vertx.keep-alive-timeout` (60s)\neventually closing idle connections.\n\nHowever, in a subset of runs (observed manually multiple times, not yet isolated to\nan exact reproducible trigger), the metric gets stuck at the pool maximum (20) and\n**does not recover even after 6+ minutes with zero further traffic** to that client.\nThis appears to be a genuine leak, not just idle connections waiting for their\nkeep-alive timeout, since 6 minutes is well beyond the 60s default keep-alive\nwindow.\n\nWe ran a scripted A/B comparison to check whether this was fixed by a specific\nQuarkus CXF version bump (suspecting the fix described in the [3.33.0 release\nnotes](https://docs.quarkiverse.io/quarkus-cxf/dev/release-notes/3.33.0.html) —\n\"receive timeout is measured starting from when the HTTP connection is ready,\nrather than from when the request is created and queued for a free connection\"):\n\n| Version | Load profile | Runs stuck (no recovery within 5 min) | Runs recovered |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| 3.26.2 | 80 threads / 30s duration | 8 / 10 | 2 / 10 (took ~285s to recover) |\n| 3.37.3 | 80 threads / 30s duration | 0 / 10 | 10 / 10 (~55-60s) |\n| 3.37.3 | 150 threads / 180s duration (~995 req/run) | 0 / 10 | 10 / 10 (~55-60s) |\n\nThis suggests something between 3.26.2 and 3.37.3 significantly reduced the\nfrequency of the leak (from an ~80% failure rate down to 0% across 20 scripted\nruns), but did not necessarily eliminate it entirely — the original manual\nreproduction of a 6+ minute permanent stuck state was observed on **3.37.3**\nas well, outside of the scripted batch runs, so we cannot yet conclusively say the\nleak is fully resolved even on the latest version, only that it appears to have\nbecome much rarer / harder to trigger with our current reproduction setup.\n\n## Question\n\nIs my assumption correct that, since version 3.30, idle and dead connections are automatically evicted by the framework after 60 seconds?","bodyHTML":"\u003ch2 dir=\"auto\"\u003eDescription\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp an independent team also hitting this in production on Quarkus CXF with VertxHttpClientHTTPConduitFactory (using RHBQ 3.27). \nOur SOAP client would stop recovering after downstream slowness/unresponsiveness: the Vert.x pool would get stuck at max, and only a pod restart cleared it. \nSwitching to URLConnectionHTTPConduitFactory avoided the issue for us.\n\nReproducer\nHere is a test in the quarkus-cxf repo that acts as a partial reproducer, based on your reproducer in ReceiveTimeoutTest: ConnectionExhaustionTest.receiveTimeoutVertxConduit() ([link](https://github.com/quarkiverse/quarkus-cxf/pull/2261))\n\nWith receive-timeout=100ms, pool size=5, 20 threads × 20 iterations:\n```\n{receive-timeout=380, The timeout of 30000 ms has been exceeded when getting a connection=20}\npool exhausted (storm): 20\n\n```\n20 out of 400 requests permanently leaked a pool slot (triggered by a timing race where DELAY ≈ receive-timeout). The companion receiveTimeoutUrlConnectionConduit() test passes cleanly — 0 leaked — confirming the bug is specific to VertxHttpClientHTTPConduitFactory.\n\nThe only way that we could reproduce the issue locally with full exhaustion was to mock a server that accepts the TCP connection, receives the full HTTP request body, but never sends any response, so that the connections are never closed.\n\nRoot cause\nVertxHttpClientHTTPConduit uses Future.timeout(), which fails the future chain but does not close the underlying request/connection or return the pool slot.\n\nQuestion\nWould the fix be to call req.reset() in the timeout failure handlers, especially in prepareResponse() and finishRequest()?   <div data-target="react-app.reactRoot"><link rel="preload" as="image" href="https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/19404874?u=c3e03669fc8feb128f76742423702c0355893655&amp;v=4&amp;size=48"/><link rel="preload" as="image" href="https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/1826249?u=a1afb52c18daa60c4976e1468faabb6db6a81a9c&amp;v=4&amp;size=48"/><link rel="preload" as="image" href="https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/26568868?s=64&amp;v=4"/><link rel="preload" as="image" href="https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/26568868?v=4&amp;size=48"/><link rel="preload" as="image" href="https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/1826249?s=64&amp;u=a1afb52c18daa60c4976e1468faabb6db6a81a9c&amp;v=4"/><meta name="react-app-name" content="issues"/><a hidden="" id="code-view-repo-link" href="/quarkiverse/quarkus-cxf" data-discover="true"></a><!--$--><!--$--><script type="application/ld+json">{"@context":"https://schema.org","@type":"DiscussionForumPosting","headline":"VertxHttpClientHTTPConduit: client connections not released after receive-timeout when the server consumes the request but does not send any response","articleBody":"## Description\n\nWhen a CXF client using the default `VertxHttpClientHTTPConduitFactory` experiences\na `receive-timeout` (`quarkus.cxf.client.\"client-name\".receive-timeout`) while under\nconcurrent load, some connections are not returned to the Vert.x HTTP connection\npool. Once enough of these accumulate, the pool becomes permanently stuck at its\nconfigured maximum (`quarkus.cxf.client.\"client-name\".vertx.connection-pool.http1-max-size`)\nand never recovers, even after all client traffic stops. New requests to the same\ndownstream client then queue indefinitely / fail, effectively blocking that CXF\nclient for the lifetime of the JVM process (only a restart recovers it).\n\nThis was investigated as a suspected root cause of a production incident where an\napplication's outbound SOAP client stopped being able to reach a downstream service\nafter a period of that downstream service being slow/unresponsive, even once the\ndownstream service recovered.\n\n## Actual behavior\n\nAcross repeated runs, on both Quarkus 3.26.2 and 3.37.3, `http_client_active_connections`\nclimbs to the configured pool max (20 in our tests) under load, as expected. In\nmost runs it recovers to 0 within roughly 55-90 seconds after load stops — consistent\nwith the default `quarkus.cxf.client.\"client-name\".vertx.keep-alive-timeout` (60s)\neventually closing idle connections.\n\nHowever, in a subset of runs (observed manually multiple times, not yet isolated to\nan exact reproducible trigger), the metric gets stuck at the pool maximum (20) and\n**does not recover even after 6+ minutes with zero further traffic** to that client.\nThis appears to be a genuine leak, not just idle connections waiting for their\nkeep-alive timeout, since 6 minutes is well beyond the 60s default keep-alive\nwindow.\n\nWe ran a scripted A/B comparison to check whether this was fixed by a specific\nQuarkus CXF version bump (suspecting the fix described in the [3.33.0 release\nnotes](https://docs.quarkiverse.io/quarkus-cxf/dev/release-notes/3.33.0.html) —\n\"receive timeout is measured starting from when the HTTP connection is ready,\nrather than from when the request is created and queued for a free connection\"):\n\n| Version | Load profile | Runs stuck (no recovery within 5 min) | Runs recovered |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| 3.26.2 | 80 threads / 30s duration | 8 / 10 | 2 / 10 (took ~285s to recover) |\n| 3.37.3 | 80 threads / 30s duration | 0 / 10 | 10 / 10 (~55-60s) |\n| 3.37.3 | 150 threads / 180s duration (~995 req/run) | 0 / 10 | 10 / 10 (~55-60s) |\n\nThis suggests something between 3.26.2 and 3.37.3 significantly reduced the\nfrequency of the leak (from an ~80% failure rate down to 0% across 20 scripted\nruns), but did not necessarily eliminate it entirely — the original manual\nreproduction of a 6+ minute permanent stuck state was observed on **3.37.3**\nas well, outside of the scripted batch runs, so we cannot yet conclusively say the\nleak is fully resolved even on the latest version, only that it appears to have\nbecome much rarer / harder to trigger with our current reproduction setup.\n\n## Question\n\nIs my assumption correct that, since version 3.30, idle and dead connections are automatically evicted by the framework after 60 seconds?","author":{"url":"https://github.com/cj19","@type":"Person","name":"cj19"},"datePublished":"2026-07-30T08:19:59Z","url":"https://github.com/quarkiverse/quarkus-cxf/issues/2240"}</script><div class="headerSpacer__q-zg6l"></div></div></div><div class="stickyHeaderSentinel__lNt767" aria-hidden="true"></div><div class="contentContainer__6-VbUZ"><div class="contentAndSidebarWrapper__RbrYO4 class="outerContainer__iY934Y"><div data-testid="issue-body" class="react-issue-body innerContainer__mxK8FR" data-hpc="true" containertiming="hpc"><h2 class="sr-only">Description</h2><div class="headerRow__U6n1NK"><div class="commentBorder__V4zML9"><div class="IssueBodyHeaderContainer__FFEvZk IssueBodyHeaderContainer_inlineAvatars__jtIrNF viewerDidNotAuthor__ZuIsgG"><div class="activityHeader__V45NcJ activityHeaderWrapper__lI1Kau"><div class="avatarContainer__JXz0Zl"><a class="avatarLink__sqWzwe prc-Link-Link-9ZwDx" data-component="Link" href="https://github.com/cj19" aria-label="@cj19&#x27;s profile"><img data-component="Avatar" class="prc-Avatar-Avatar-0xaUi" alt="@cj19" width="24" height="24" style="--avatarSize-regular:24px" src="https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/19404874?u=c3e03669fc8feb128f76742423702c0355893655&amp;v=4&amp;size=48" data-testid="github-avatar"/></a></div><div class="narrowViewportWrapper__C5cNkS narrowViewportWrapper__Uis-SQ"><div class="titleSection__PUvpxr"><a class="authorLoginLink__51Riy1 prc-Link-Link-9ZwDx" data-component="Link" href="https://github.com/cj19" data-testid="issue-body-header-author">cj19</a></div><div class="footer__7ecx1m footerSection__kzv2bq"><span>opened </span><a class="dateLink__IC75fj prc-Link-Link-9ZwDx" data-component="Link" href="https://github.com/quarkiverse/quarkus-cxf/issues/2240#issue-5017734428" data-testid="issue-body-header-link"><relative-time class="RelativeTime__isT-DQ" data-component="RelativeTime">on Jul 30, 2026</relative-time></a></div></div><div class="narrowViewportWrapper__C5cNkS actionsWrapper__rTb725"><div class="edits__KjKzVq editsSection__3L_O_u"></div><div class="badgesSection__IjiaLx"><div class="badgeGroup__ZvfmMk"></div><div class="actionsSection__QKxX29"><button data-component="IconButton" type="button" aria-haspopup="true" aria-expanded="false" tabindex="0" class="prc-Button-ButtonBase-9n-Xk prc-Button-IconButton-fyge7" data-loading="false" data-no-visuals="true" data-size="small" data-variant="invisible" aria-labelledby="_R_fqlmkjmr9mm5_" id="_R_qlmkjmr9mm5_"><svg data-component="Octicon" aria-hidden="true" focusable="false" class="octicon octicon-kebab-horizontal" viewBox="0 0 16 16" width="16" height="16" fill="currentColor" display="inline-block" overflow="visible" style="vertical-align:text-bottom"><path d="M8 9a1.5 1.5 0 1 0 0-3 1.5 1.5 0 0 0 0 3ZM1.5 9a1.5 1.5 0 1 0 0-3 1.5 1.5 0 0 0 0 3Zm13 0a1.5 1.5 0 1 0 0-3 1.5 1.5 0 0 0 0 3Z"></path></svg></button><span class="prc-TooltipV2-Tooltip-tLeuB" data-direction="s" data-component="Tooltip" aria-hidden="true" id="_R_fqlmkjmr9mm5_">Issue body actions</span></div></div></div></div></div><div class="IssueBody__1iGjHw" id="issue-body-viewer" data-testid="issue-body-viewer"><div data-testid="markdown-body" data-team-hovercards-enabled="true" class="markdown-body" data-turbolinks="false"><div class="markdown-body safe-html-box__Uwc6Al"><h2 dir="auto">Description</h2>
<p dir="auto">When a CXF client using the default <code class="notranslate">VertxHttpClientHTTPConduitFactory</code> experiences<br>
a <code class="notranslate">receive-timeout</code> (<code class="notranslate">quarkus.cxf.client."client-name".receive-timeout</code>) while under<br>
concurrent load, some connections are not returned to the Vert.x HTTP connection<br>
pool. Once enough of these accumulate, the pool becomes permanently stuck at its<br>
configured maximum (<code class="notranslate">quarkus.cxf.client."client-name".vertx.connection-pool.http1-max-size</code>)<br>
and never recovers, even after all client traffic stops. New requests to the same<br>
downstream client then queue indefinitely / fail, effectively blocking that CXF<br>
client for the lifetime of the JVM process (only a restart recovers it).</p>
<p dir="auto">This was investigated as a suspected root cause of a production incident where an<br>
application's outbound SOAP client stopped being able to reach a downstream service<br>
after a period of that downstream service being slow/unresponsive, even once the<br>
downstream service recovered.</p>
<h2 dir="auto">Actual behavior</h2>
<p dir="auto">Across repeated runs, on both Quarkus 3.26.2 and 3.37.3, <code class="notranslate">http_client_active_connections</code><br>
climbs to the configured pool max (20 in our tests) under load, as expected. In<br>
most runs it recovers to 0 within roughly 55-90 seconds after load stops — consistent<br>
with the default <code class="notranslate">quarkus.cxf.client."client-name".vertx.keep-alive-timeout</code> (60s)<br>
eventually closing idle connections.</p>
<p dir="auto">However, in a subset of runs (observed manually multiple times, not yet isolated to<br>
an exact reproducible trigger), the metric gets stuck at the pool maximum (20) and<br>
<strong>does not recover even after 6+ minutes with zero further traffic</strong> to that client.<br>
This appears to be a genuine leak, not just idle connections waiting for their<br>
keep-alive timeout, since 6 minutes is well beyond the 60s default keep-alive<br>
window.</p>
<p dir="auto">We ran a scripted A/B comparison to check whether this was fixed by a specific<br>
Quarkus CXF version bump (suspecting the fix described in the <a href="https://docs.quarkiverse.io/quarkus-cxf/dev/release-notes/3.33.0.html" rel="nofollow">3.33.0 release<br>
notes</a> —<br>
"receive timeout is measured starting from when the HTTP connection is ready,<br>
rather than from when the request is created and queued for a free connection"):</p>
<markdown-accessiblity-table><table role="table">
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Version</th>
<th>Load profile</th>
<th>Runs stuck (no recovery within 5 min)</th>
<th>Runs recovered</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>3.26.2</td>
<td>80 threads / 30s duration</td>
<td>8 / 10</td>
<td>2 / 10 (took ~285s to recover)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>3.37.3</td>
<td>80 threads / 30s duration</td>
<td>0 / 10</td>
<td>10 / 10 (~55-60s)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>3.37.3</td>
<td>150 threads / 180s duration (~995 req/run)</td>
<td>0 / 10</td>
<td>10 / 10 (~55-60s)</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></markdown-accessiblity-table>
<p dir="auto">This suggests something between 3.26.2 and 3.37.3 significantly reduced the<br>
frequency of the leak (from an ~80% failure rate down to 0% across 20 scripted<br>
runs), but did not necessarily eliminate it entirely — the original manual<br>
reproduction of a 6+ minute permanent stuck state was observed on <strong>3.37.3</strong><br>
as well, outside of the scripted batch runs, so we cannot yet conclusively say the<br>
leak is fully resolved even on the latest version, only that it appears to have<br>
become much rarer / harder to trigger with our current reproduction setup.</p>
<h2 dir="auto">Question</h2>
<p dir="auto">Is my assumption correct that, since version 3.30, idle and dead connections are automatically evicted by the framework after 60 seconds?</p></div></div><div class="IssueBodyTaskList__-FlQre"><div role="toolbar" aria-label="Reactions" class="d-flex gap-1 flex-wrap"></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><!--$--><div <p dir="auto">Is my assumption correct that, since version 3.30, idle and dead connections are automatically evicted by the framework after 60 seconds?</p>
</blockquote>
<p dir="auto">Quarkus CXF just configures the pool and the eviction is implemented in and triggered by Vert.x.</p>
<p dir="auto">Quarkus CXF 3.26.2 used Vert.x 4.5.20,<br>
Quarkus CXF 3.37 uses Vert.x 4.5.28</p>
<p dir="auto">You may want to ask your agent whether something changed there.</p>
<p dir="auto">From what you say, I have the impression, the eviction may work as expected, but the problem is rather with receive-timeouted connections being still seen as busy and not ready to serve further requests. When this eventually happens to all connections from the pool, then no requests at all get served.</p>
<p dir="auto">It would be great if we could focus on the latest Quarkus CXF version, currently 3.38.0, or the lastest in the 3.33 stream. Anything else is unsupported.</p>
<p dir="auto">It would be great to be able to reproduce it.</p>
<p dir="auto">Were your "scripted" and "manual" tests run against the same backend?<br>
How is the receive timeout simulated on the server side in the setup where you saw the issue?</p>
<p dir="auto">"150 threads / 180s duration" means you were hitting the same client from 150 threads in the same or different JVM?</p>
<p dir="auto">How do you know the clients got receive timeout? Any stacktraces would be very useful.</p></div></div><div role="toolbar" aria-label="Reactions" class="d-flex gap-1 flex-wrap"></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></li><li <p dir="auto">Not sure any of those matches what you look for.</p>
<blockquote>
<p dir="auto">all client traffic stops. New requests to the same downstream client then queue indefinitely / fail, effectively blocking that CXF client for the lifetime of the JVM process (only a restart recovers it).</p>
</blockquote>
<p dir="auto">What does the app do in that state? Client calls hit <code class="notranslate">quarkus.cxf.client.worker-dispatch-timeout</code> with a message like <code class="notranslate">Unable to dispatch SOAP client call within x ms on a worker thread due to worker thread pool exhaustion.</code>?</p></div></div><div role="toolbar" aria-label="Reactions" class="d-flex gap-1 flex-wrap"></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></li><li data-team-hovercards-enabled="true" class="markdown-body" data-turbolinks="false"><div class="markdown-body safe-html-box__Uwc6Al"><blockquote>
<p dir="auto">What does the app do in that state? Client calls hit <code class="notranslate">quarkus.cxf.client.worker-dispatch-timeout</code> with a message like <code class="notranslate">Unable to dispatch SOAP client call within x ms on a worker thread due to worker thread pool exhaustion.</code>?</p>
</blockquote>
<p dir="auto">It's not a worker-dispatch-timeout message, it's a generically-wrapped TimeoutIOException, and I don't yet have visibility into what (if anything) is thrown once the pool is fully stuck versus during the initial receive-timeouts.</p>
<p dir="auto">Actually my service wraps the cxfClient.send(...) call in a try - catch block that catches any RuntimeException and rethrows a custom exception.<br>
So whatever CXF/Vert.x actually threw (confirmed via logs to be VertxHttpClientHTTPConduit$TimeoutIOException, i.e. the receive-timeout firing) gets logged once at WARN, then replaced with a generic message before reaching the caller. That's why the JMeter .jtl logs just show a plain SOAP fault / HTTP 500 for every failure — the app's own exception handling erases the distinction between a normal receive-timeout and whatever exception (if any) fires once the pool is actually stuck.</p>
<p dir="auto">If I can provide any information, just let me know.</p></div></div><div role="toolbar" aria-label="Reactions" class="d-flex gap-1 flex-wrap"></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></li><li data-wrapper-timeline-id="IC_kwDOElz7Sc8AAAABNIJf7w"><div class="timelineElement__l_1Q03 nonLeadingElement_inlineAvatars__g_btmU"><div actions</span></div></div></div></div></div><div class="Body__scgEjp IssueCommentBody__DGWd7U"><div data-testid="markdown-body" data-team-hovercards-enabled="true" class="markdown-body" data-turbolinks="false"><div class="markdown-body safe-html-box__Uwc6Al"><p dir="auto">We’re an independent team also hitting this in production on Quarkus CXF with VertxHttpClientHTTPConduitFactory (using RHBQ 3.27).<br>
Our SOAP client would stop recovering after downstream slowness/unresponsiveness: the Vert.x pool would get stuck at max, and only a pod restart cleared it.<br>
Switching to URLConnectionHTTPConduitFactory avoided the issue for us.</p>
<p dir="auto">Reproducer<br>
Here is a test in the quarkus-cxf repo that acts as a partial reproducer, based on your reproducer in ReceiveTimeoutTest: ConnectionExhaustionTest.receiveTimeoutVertxConduit() (<a href="https://github.com/quarkiverse/quarkus-cxf/pull/2261" data-hovercard-type="pull_request" data-hovercard-url="/quarkiverse/quarkus-cxf/pull/2261/hovercard">link</a>)</p>
<p dir="auto">With receive-timeout=100ms, pool size=5, 20 threads × 20 iterations:</p>
<div class="snippet-clipboard-content notranslate position-relative overflow-auto" data-snippet-clipboard-copy-content="{receive-timeout=380, The timeout of 30000 ms has been exceeded when getting a connection=20}
pool exhausted (storm): 20
"><pre class="notranslate"><code class="notranslate">{receive-timeout=380, The timeout of 30000 ms has been exceeded when getting a connection=20}
pool exhausted (storm): 20

</code></pre></div>
<p dir="auto">20 out of 400 requests permanently leaked a pool slot (triggered by a timing race where DELAY ≈ receive-timeout). The companion receiveTimeoutUrlConnectionConduit() test passes cleanly — 0 leaked — confirming the bug is specific to VertxHttpClientHTTPConduitFactory.</p>
<p dir="auto">The only way that we could reproduce the issue locally with full exhaustion was to mock a server that accepts the TCP connection, receives the full HTTP request body, but never sends any response, so that the connections are never closed.</p>
<p dir="auto">Root cause<br>
VertxHttpClientHTTPConduit uses Future.timeout(), which fails the future chain but does not close the underlying request/connection or return the pool slot.</p>
<p dir="auto">Question<br>
Would the fix be to call req.reset() in the timeout failure handlers, especially in prepareResponse() and finishRequest()?</p></div></div><div role="toolbar" aria-label="Reactions" class="d-flex gap-1 flex-wrap"></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></li><li data-wrapper-timeline-id="IC_kwDOElz7Sc8AAAABQDSWJw"><div class="timelineElement__l_1Q03 nonLeadingElement_inlineAvatars__g_btmU"><div class="Box__9aTioE"><div data-testid="timeline-divider-IC_kwDOElz7Sc8AAAABQDSWJw" class="Box__swapE6 Box_inlineAvatars__OXEjSV"><div data-testid="timeline-divider-connector"
